\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\lambda)
.
The grammar
\mathcal{G}_{\text{bow}}
can among others generate DAGs similar to garlands and rainbows.
To generate an arbitrary long DAG looking like a garland,
the set of meta-states

\mathcal{Q}=\{\varnothing,p,pq\}
suffices.
If, however,
\mathcal{G}_{\text{bow}}
generates a DAG looking like a rainbow,
it repeats the rule with the vertex label
\mathtt{o}

an unbounded number of times.
9
9

9

Allowing pennants spanning more than one vertex is possible, too.
With
\mathcal{Q}=\{pq^{n}\mid p,q\in N\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}\}

the bow(s) for the pennants in a DAG
G_{garland}\in L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})
can span the maximum of
n
vertices.

Such a rainbow DAG in
L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})
, with the length of the bow(s) limited,
can only be generated by a deterministic DAG grammar if the grammar’s language is finite.
But, a finite language cannot include DAGs with
n
-sized bows of arbitrary length.
On the other hand, any grammar generating garland DAGs of unbounded length will
also include the rainbow DAGs due to the possibility to swap the edges (Lemma
2.11
).
And, according to Lemma
4.6
, we can swap the edges
since the edge labels (the nonterminals) must be repeated for a DAG of unbounded length
and distinct edges labeled with the same nonterminal in a derivation DAG can be swapped.
Thus, restricting the language to
L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G}_{\text{bow}})
by above given
\mathcal{Q}

results in a language not in
\mathit{RDL^{\text{det}}}\!
,
completing the proof.
∎

Again, swapping (Lemma
2.11
) would allow us
to generate unbounded rainbows, so we conclude:
restricting a grammar by a set of meta-states
can prevent the swapping operation.

Corollary 7.2

(Swapping in
\mathtt{FD}
)
.

For a grammar
\mathcal{G}
with its
L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})\in\mathtt{FD}\>\backslash\>\mathtt{FID}
holds:

•

\exists\>\lfloor D\rfloor\!\in\!L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G}):\lfloor D(e_{0}\bowtie e_{1})\rfloor\notin L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})

•

|L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})|<|L(\mathcal{G})|

•

L(\mathcal{G})=\{\>\lfloor D(e_{0}\bowtie e_{1})\rfloor\>|\>D(e_{0}\bowtie e_{1})\in L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})\>\}

•

L(\mathcal{G})\in\mathtt{ID}

Contrary to
\mathtt{FID}
-grammars on whose derivation DAGs swapping is always allowed,
the derivation DAGs of grammars generating languages in
L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})\in\mathtt{FD}\backslash\mathtt{FID}

the swapping operation is restricted by the given set of meta-states
\mathcal{Q}
.
Restricting a grammar by allowing only certain meta-states
corresponds to restricting swapping on the derivation DAGs.
Via those restrictions, DAGs are lost which cannot be accepted without the missing meta-states
resulting in a language with less graphs.
The transitive closure of the swapping operation on the language
L^{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{G})

returns the original language
L(\mathcal{G})
which must be in the class
\mathtt{ID}
.

For free – by
\mathtt{FD}
definition – we can observe the closure properties valid for DFAs,
since
\mathtt{FD}
languages are recognized by DFAs.

Observation 7.3

(
\mathtt{FD}
– Closure under Union and Intersection)
.

The language class
\mathtt{FD}
is closed under union and intersection.

8
Conclusion

We have defined the DAG language classes
\mathtt{ID}
and
\mathtt{FD}

and characterized them.
By imposing the set of meta-states as a given restriction,
we additionally have defined
\mathtt{FD}
, which intersects with
\mathit{RDL}
but is not a subset of it – adding to expressiveness.
For languages in
\mathtt{FID}
and
\mathtt{FD}
,
we proved that it is possible to construct a classical string automaton recognizing the language.
Analysing
\mathtt{ID}
further, we expect similarities with the Chomsky hierarchy.

Pushdown Conjecture
.

Analogous to languages in
\mathtt{FD}
being recognized by a finite state automaton,
we conjecture all languages in
\mathtt{ID}
to be recognized by a pushdown automaton.

The FSA construction by meta-states,
could be applied not only to the top-down deterministic version, but also to the plain regular DAG automaton.
By dropping the determinism restriction, similar to dropping
the planarity restriction
[
33
]
imposed
in
[
22
]
, possibly the NP-completeness for the membership problem could be tackled.
Imposing useful restrictions to be provided by the set of meta-states
\mathcal{Q}
,
as the language class
\mathtt{FD}
requires it,
is the task for more application centric research,
like semantic NLP parsing
[
30
,
4
]
.

9
Appendix
